Balancing Criteria

       2.10.3 - Long-term Effectiveness and Permanence

This criterion evaluates the effectiveness of the alternatives in protecting human health and the
environment over the long-term, once the RAOs have been achieved, including the adequacy and
reliability of controls to address residual risk. This criterion also incorporates an evaluation of
climate resilience.

Alternative 2 would provide a high level of effectiveness and permanence since DDx-
contaminated floodplain and riverbank soils would be removed to a concentration of 1 ppm.
Clean fill would be placed over the excavated areas and riverbanks would be stabilized to
prevent erosion. Unacceptable risk to ecological receptors would be removed through
excavation, off-site disposal of contaminated soils and stabilization of the riverbanks. Any
residual risk would be removed with the implementation of Alternative 2. In addition, climate
resiliency would be part of Alternative 2. The potential exists to have storms producing large
flooding events, and to address this issue, the use of robust erosion control measures will be
required.

       2.10.4 - Reduction of Toxicity, Mobility, or Volume through Treatment

This criterion evaluates the anticipated performance of the treatment technologies that may be
included as part of a remedy, including the degree of expected reductions in toxicity, mobility or
volume of the contaminants through treatment, the degree to which the treatment is irreversible,
and the type and quantity of residuals remaining after treatment.

Alternative 2 will not reduce the toxicity or volume through treatment since contaminated soils
from floodplains and riverbanks would be removed and disposed off-site in an approved landfill
without treatment. Based upon pre-design sampling, some excavated material may require
stabilization prior to disposal in a non-hazardous landfill, which would reduce contaminant
mobility. For cost estimating purposes, it was assumed 2,000 cubic yards of contaminated soils
would require stabilization.

       2.10.5 - Short-term Effectiveness

This criterion addresses the length of time needed to implement the remedy and any adverse
impacts that may be posed to the community, remediation workers, and the environment during
construction of the remedy until RAOs are achieved.

The low level DDx contamination should not pose a risk to the community or workers during the
excavation and engineering controls will be put in place to ensure unacceptable risks are
minimized. Construction equipment, dust, noise and the transport of excavated contaminated
soils through the community could potentially pose a risk; and health and safety plans, including
a transportation plan, will be required to minimize the risk. In addition, remedial activities would
result in substantial environmental impacts to wetlands due to tree removal and clearing and



Velsicol Chemical OU3 ROD                                                                   Page 33
grubbing activities. The riverbanks will also be impacted due to clearing and grubbing activities,
and it is unlikely that restoration activities will immediately restore the riverbanks to their
original condition, considering the existing slopes are steep and vegetation in the remediation
areas is mature. It is anticipated that the riverbanks will be restored with a more gradual slope
and armored to prevent erosion.

       2.10.6 - Implementability

This criterion assesses the technical and administrative feasibility of a remedy from design
through construction, including the availability of required goods and services. Technical
feasibility considers the ability to construct and operate a technology and its reliability, the ease
of undertaking additional remedial actions, and the ability to monitor the effectiveness of a
remedy. Administrative feasibility considers the ability to obtain approvals from other parties or
agencies and the extent of required coordination with other parties or agencies.

Soil excavation with off-site disposal is an established, field-proven technology. The equipment
would need to be scaled accordingly to access the work areas and minimize impacts to private
properties. The remediation will require access from the individual property owners. Access
constraints could be an issue for excavation, especially at the riverbanks. Additional challenges
could be large trucks removing contaminated soil and clean backfill through private property.
Restoration will likely be required for access points.

       2.10.7 - Cost

This criterion considers the estimated capital costs, annual O&M costs, and the total present
worth of the capital and O&M costs, including long-term monitoring. The total present worth
cost is calculated using a discount rate that takes into account the time value of money.

The cost for Alternative 2 is estimated to be $6,656,781 million dollars with an estimated
accuracy of +50% to -30%. It is expected that one construction season will be needed to
complete the work and no operation and maintenance costs will be required. Table 5 shows a
detailed cost analysis for Alternative 2.

Modifying Criteria

       2.10.8 - State/Support Agency Acceptance

This criterion considers whether the state support agency supports the preferred alternative
presented in the Proposed Plan and concurs with the Selected Remedy.

EGLE has indicated that it intends to concur with the Selected Remedy for the OU3 remedy at
the Velsicol Chemical Site. The concurrence letter will be included in the AR upon receipt.

       2.10.9 - Community Acceptance

preferred alternative and other alternatives identified in the Proposed Plan.

EPA received comments during the public comment period and these comments are addressed in
the Responsiveness Summary. No comments were received in opposition to Alternative 2.

2.11 - Principal Threat Waste

The NCP establishes an expectation that EPA will use treatment to address the principal threats
posed by a site wherever practicable (NCP Section 300.430(a)(1)(iii)(A)). The principal threat
concept is applied to the characterization of “source material” at a Superfund site. Source
material is material that includes or contains hazardous substances, pollutants or contaminants
that act as a reservoir for migration of contaminants to groundwater, surface water or air, or acts
as a source for direct exposure. EPA has defined principal threat wastes as those source materials
considered to be highly toxic or highly mobile that generally cannot be reliably contained or
would present a significant risk to human health or the environment should exposure occur.

EPA has not identified any principal threat wastes at OU3. All floodplain soils, stream bank soils
and sediment are at low concentrations and do not present significant risk to human health and
the environment. Excluding the ingestion of bottom feeding fish, no human health risk exists
from the DDx or any other COCs. Unacceptable risk exists to ecological receptors but not at
significant levels.

2.12 - Selected Remedy

EPA’s Selected Remedy for addressing the contamination at the Site is Alternative 2 - Removal
and Backfill/Cover or Riverbank Stabilization of OU3 Floodplains and Riverbank Soils.

       2.12.1 Summary of Rationale for the Selected Remedy

EPA believes that the Selected Remedy, Alternative 2, provides the best balance of the
evaluation criteria among all the alternatives. EPA initially evaluated a containment alternative
but determined that it would not be protective over the long-term, especially to ecological
receptors. Alternative 2 is protective of human health and the environment, meets all federal and
state ARARs, achieves the RAOs for this remedial action in a reasonable timeframe and at a
reasonable cost, is straightforward in its implementation, is effective in the long-term and
includes permanent measures. Alternative 2 will also allow the impacted properties to be used
for their current and reasonably anticipated future land use and over the long term, DDx levels in
fish tissue will be reduced.




Velsicol Chemical OU3 ROD                                                                  Page 35
       2.12.2 Summary of Selected Remedy

The major components of the Selected Remedy for OU3 at the Velsicol Chemical Site are as
follows:
 x   Further survey and sample to completely delineate the lateral and horizontal footprint of
     contaminated riverbank soils (including the removal depth) and to confirm the removal
     depth in Floodplain (FP) 0.5, Floodplain 1, Floodplain 1.1 and the athletic fields.
 x   Remove approximately 2 feet thickness of riverbank soils to achieve an average bank
     height of 5 feet along the entirety of the northern and southern banks to promote bank
     stability. See Figure 8 for the proposed remediation footprint.
 x   Remove an average depth of 1.5 feet of the floodplains. See Figure 8 for the proposed
     remediation footprint.
 x   Total contaminated soils excavated is estimated to be 20,350 cubic yards with 2,000 cubic
     yards requiring stabilization prior to off-site disposal.
 x   Clear the floodplains of vegetation prior to excavation and dispose of off-site.
 x   Transport and dispose of soil at a permitted RCRA Subtitle D landfill where all soils will
     be characterized as RCRA non-hazardous.
 x   Backfill the floodplains with clean soil, grade, and plant the floodplains.
 x   Stabilize the riverbank using hardened shorelines, rootwads, grading, and vegetation.
 x   Regrade (as needed) and seed all access roads and staging areas used during the
     remedial action to prevent soil erosion.

       2.12.3 Summary of Estimated Remedy Costs

The estimated cost of implementing the Selected Remedy is $6,656,781. A detailed cost estimate
for the Selected Remedy, Alternative 2, is included in Table 5. The information in the cost
estimates is based on the best available information regarding the anticipated scope of the
remedial alternatives. This is an order-of-magnitude engineering cost estimate that is expected to
be within +50 to -30 percent of the actual project cost. Changes in the cost elements could result
based on pre-design sampling within the floodplains and stream banks. Major changes in the
remedial action cost may be documented in the form of a memorandum in the Administrative
Record file, an Explanation of Significant Differences, or a ROD Amendment.

       2.12.4 Expected Outcomes of Selected Remedy

The primary objectives for the Selected Remedy are to reduce the potential for exposure to DDx
by ecological receptors by removing contaminated soil from floodplains and stream banks. In
addition, through removing contaminated soils, the spread of DDx contamination downstream by
erosion and runoff will be prevented and DDx levels in fish tissue will be reduced.




Velsicol Chemical OU3 ROD                                                                 Page 36
It is expected that construction will take less than 12 months and the RAOs will be reached once
construction is complete. OU3 is expected to be available for unrestricted current and reasonably
anticipated future land use as a result of the remedy.

Anticipated Community Impacts

Implementation of the Selected Remedy will improve DDx levels in fish tissue over time and
reduce to acceptable levels the current and future risks to human health and the environment
posed by OU3. The remedy will do this by excavating and disposing off-site DDx contaminated
materials that pose an unacceptable risk, from floodplains and stream banks within OU3. Access
from owners of floodplains and the St. Louis School Board during implementation of the remedy
will be required and some short-term impacts to the landowners could occur due to excavation
and truck activities.

2.13 - Statutory Determinations

Under CERCLA Section 121 and the NCP, the lead agency must select remedies that are
protective of human health and the environment, comply with applicable or relevant and
appropriate requirements (unless a statutory waiver is justified), are cost-effective, and utilize
permanent solutions and alternative treatment technologies or resource recovery technologies to
the maximum extent practicable. In addition, CERCLA includes a preference for remedies that
employ treatment that permanently and significantly reduces the toxicity, mobility or volume of
hazardous wastes as a principal element and a bias against off-site disposal of untreated wastes.
The following sections discuss how the Selected Remedy meets these statutory requirements.

Protection of Human Health and the Environment

The Selected Remedy provides overall protection of human health and the environment from
DDx-impacted soils within floodplains and streambanks located in OU3. Protection of human
health and the environment will be achieved through excavation and off-site disposal of DDx-
contaminated material from four floodplains and streambanks within the area designated as OU3.
The Selected Remedy will reduce exposure levels to protective ARAR- or risk-based cleanup
levels, reducing risks to within EPA’s generally acceptable risk range of 10-4 to 10-6 for
carcinogenic risk and below the HI of 1 for non-carcinogens. The Selected Remedy also will
provide protection to ecological receptors.

No unacceptable short-term risks are anticipated by implementation of the remedy. Some short-
term risks will be created by excavation and transportation of contaminated soil, but these risks
can be minimized through proper mitigative measures during construction. For example, EPA
will work with the local community to develop a transportation plan that will strive to minimize
adverse impacts related to truck traffic during floodplain and streambank cleanup.

Compliance with Applicable or Relevant and Appropriate Requirements

The Selected Remedy will comply with federal and state ARARs. Compliance with ARARs is
discussed above in Section 2.10 and the ARARS for this action are provided in Table 4. Table 4



Velsicol Chemical OU3 ROD                                                                  Page 37
also includes information regarding TBCs that do not constitute ARARs but will be
appropriately considered during implementation of the remedy.

Cost-Effectiveness

In EPA's judgment, the Selected Remedy is cost-effective and represents a reasonable value for
the money to be spent. In making this determination, the following definition was used: “A
remedy shall be cost-effective if its costs are proportional to its overall effectiveness.” [NCP
Section 300.430(f)(I)(ii)(D)]. Overall effectiveness was evaluated by assessing three of the five
balancing criteria in combination (long-term effectiveness and permanence; reduction in toxicity,
mobility, and volume through treatment; and short-term effectiveness). Overall effectiveness was
then compared to costs to determine cost-effectiveness. The relationship of the overall
effectiveness of this remedial alternative was determined to be proportional to its costs and hence
this alternative represents a reasonable value for the money to be spent.

Utilization of Permanent Solutions and Alternative Treatment Technologies (or Resource
Recovery Technologies) to the Maximum Extent Practicable

EPA has determined that the Selected Remedy, Alternative 2, represents the maximum extent to
which permanent solutions and treatment technologies can be utilized in a practicable manner at
OU3. EPA has determined that the Selected Remedy, Alternative 2, provides the best balance of
tradeoffs in terms of the five balancing criteria, while also considering the statutory preference
for treatment as a principal element and bias against off-site disposal of untreated wastes and
considering state and community acceptance.

Alternative 2 provides long-term and permanent protection due to the excavation of floodplains
and streambanks to the 1 ppm DDx cleanup criteria.

No unacceptable short-term risks are anticipated by implementation of the remedy. Some short-
term risks will be created during implementation of the remedy, but these risks can be minimized
through proper mitigative measures during construction. There are no implementability issues as
the remediation technologies are regularly used at similar sites throughout the country.

Preference for Treatment as a Principal Element

Alternative 2 does not satisfy the preference for treatment as a principal element because soils
will be excavated from the floodplains and streambanks contain low concentrations of DDx and
no principal threat waste has been identified within the floodplains or streambanks. Treatment of
these soils containing low concentrations of DDx would not be cost effective. Some excavated
soils may require stabilization prior to disposal in a non-hazardous landfill, which would reduce
contaminant mobility. For cost estimating purposes, it was assumed 2,000 cubic yards of
contaminated soils would require stabilization.




Velsicol Chemical OU3 ROD                                                                  Page 38
Five-Year Review Requirements

Because this remedy will not result in hazardous substances, pollutants, or contaminants
remaining on-site above levels that allow for unlimited use and unrestricted exposure, a statutory
review will not be conducted within five years after initiation of remedial action. However five-
year reviews will be required for other operable units and a site-wide protectiveness will be
evaluated which will include OU3.

2.14 - Documentation of Significant Changes

The Proposed Plan for the OU3 was issued for public comment from July 15, 2022 to August 13,
2022. A virtual public meeting was held on July 26, 2022 where public comments were accepted.
The Proposed Plan identified Alternative 2 as the Preferred Alternative. CERCLA Section
117(b) and NCP Section 300.430(f)(5)(iii) require an explanation of any significant changes
from the remedy presented in the Proposed Plan that was published for public comment. EPA
reviewed all written and verbal comments submitted during the public comment period. It was
determined that no significant changes to the remedy, as originally identified in the Proposed
Plan, were necessary or appropriate.




Velsicol Chemical OU3 ROD                                                                 Page 39
                PART III – RESPONSIVENESS SUMMARY
In accordance with CERCLA Section 117, 42 U.S.C. Section 9617, EPA released the Proposed
Plan and Administrative Record for OU3 on July 15, 2022 and held a public comment period
from July 15, 2022 through August 13, 2022, to allow interested parties to comment on the
Proposed Plan. Due to COVID-19 restrictions, EPA held a virtual public meeting through
Microsoft Teams. A court reporter was present during the meeting to provide a transcript of the
meeting and record public comments.

Public comments received during the Velsicol OU3 Public Comment Period

1) Jane Jelenek (written comment) – I am glad to know more sampling will take place on the
athletic fields with potential new excavation if needed.

Response: Additional sampling will take place within the high school athletic fields and on the
streambanks adjacent to the athletic fields during the pre-design phase and, based on the
sampling results, additional remediation may be required.

2) Brittany Bayless Fremion (written comment) – I am happy that additional sampling will take
place to better define the floodplain contamination as well as include PFAS.

Response: Additional sampling is necessary to further define contaminated floodplains and
streambanks contamination. Even though we do not expect to see per- and polyfluoroalkyl
substances (PFAS) present in the floodplains or streambanks, some sampling will occur to
determine if they are present.

3) Scott Cornelius (verbal comment) - Yes, thank you. Adrian, I just wanted to end with
thanking you and Tom and everybody behind the scenes for putting this together. It was very
informative. I learned a lot of things that were not in other documents that I have read, which is
always good. I will be submitting comments in writing at a later date after we take into all this
information. And I would encourage everybody else to write in their comments because a
comment, being a former project manager, I like to see comments supporting as well as
comments against or with concerns. Or if you think you know things are good; everybody needs
to hear those comments too, so don't be shy. Submit your comments. Thank you.

Response: EPA appreciates the comment and encourages the public to become involved in the
public participation aspects of the Velsicol Chemical Site cleanup activities.

4) Brittany Bayless Fremion (written comment) – Climate resiliency is another important
aspect of this work, as is consideration of how the remedy may influence future uses of the river
and floodplain properties.




Velsicol Chemical OU3 ROD                                                                  Page 40
Response: EPA will be following the Agency’s Climate Change Adaptation Implementation
Plan as we move forward in evaluating climate change and how it will influence Site remedies.
Some of the tasks will include the following:

   1. Identify existing Superfund program processes (such as remedial
      investigations/feasibility studies, records of decision, remedial designs/remedial actions
      and five-year reviews) in which climate change-related measures may be integrated to
      ensure continuing protectiveness of current and future remedies.
   2. Develop a protocol for evaluating and ensuring remedy protectiveness on a site-specific
      basis.
   3. Produce fact sheets specific to the types of remediation systems most likely to be affected
      by climate change, to help project decision-makers identify potential system
      vulnerabilities and resilience measures.
   4. Develop and implement in-person and Web-based training to help EPA regional staff,
      cleanup contractors and other stakeholders plan and implement remedies that are resilient
      to weather and climate changes.
   5. Develop site-specific vulnerability assessment technical support that provides forward-
      looking understanding of climate impacts, documents existing resilience measures, and
      recommends adaptation measures.

More information on climate resiliency can be found at the following EPA web page:

   x   https://www.epa.gov/superfund/superfund-climate-resilience


5) Anonymous (written comment) – A commenter stated displeasure with taxpayers funding the
Velsicol Chemical cleanup.

Response: Unfortunately, Velsicol Chemical Corporation has gone through bankruptcy and the
St. Louis, Michigan, Site received approximately $18 million from the bankruptcy settlement. No
further responsible parties have been identified, and due to the failure of the initial remedy that
Velsicol implemented, EPA and the Michigan Department of Environment, Great Lakes and
Energy are responsible to fund the cleanup.

X - Potentially complete exposure pathway identified                                                                                                                                                                          Human Health Conceptual Exposure Model
(1) Floodplain soil was evaluated for five segments of the Pine River: DS-1 Zone-1, DS-1 Zone-2, DS-1 Zone-3, DS-1.25A, and DS-1.25B.                                                                                                           Velsicol Operable Unit 3
(2) Floodplain soil at athletic fields were evaluated for two exposure areas: 1) Athletic Field A - Practice fields and 2) Athletic Field B - Field that is more often visited                                                       St Louis, Gratiot County, Michigan
 and used by ball players including children.
(3) River bank soil was evaluated for two segments along the Pine River: DS-1 Zone 1 along Floodplain 1 and DS-1 Zone 2 along Floodplains 1.1, 1.2 and 1.3.
(4) Surface water in the Pine River was evaluated for two river segments: DS-1 and DS-1.25.
(5) Sediment (including both transect and deposition sediment) was evaluated for five segments of the Pine River: DS-1 Zone-1, DS-1 Zone-2, DS-1 Zone-3, DS-1.25A, and DS-1.25B.
(6) Fish in the Pine River was evaluated for two fish types: 1) Predatory fish and 2) Bottom feeder fish.
(7) Groundskeeper exposures were not quantified in the risk assessment because the students and coach exposures are more conservative and are protective of the groundskeeper.
